Figure 4:
The orbit
O(\mathbf{n}_{0})
can be represented as a single chain due to the simplification of the involutions
T_{\mathbf{a}}(\mathbf{n}_{0})
, which only relate odd multiple of the condensates amongst themselves. Removing the condensate labels
\pm\mathbf{n}_{0}
(gray dots) one is left with the severed set
\mathcal{M}
defined in (
113
).

We have seen that a generic label
\mathbf{n^{\prime}}
that is not related to
\mathbf{n}
via the maps (
106
) forms a separate orbit
O(\mathbf{n}^{\prime})
. Indeed, (
112
) is a way to partition the whole set of modes into disjoint
cosets
. Inspired by this partition, we now abandon the notation
\mathbf{n}\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}
, and relabel the modes so as to reflect the splitting into disjoint pieces. We first introduce a coordinate system and discuss the diagonalisation procedure for all the generic bi-chains
O(\mathbf{n})
except for
\mathcal{M}
, and later comment on the remaining severed chain, for will need a separate (but fully parallel) diagonalisation procedure.

For any generic orbit
O(\mathbf{n})
such that
\mathbf{n}\notin\mathcal{M}
, we define the label
\mathbf{s}
as the
coset representative
that uniquely identifies which bi-chain a given mode belongs to. Next, one identifies a binary parameter
\varepsilon
and an integer
m
that together “coordinatise” the position on a given bi-chain. Then, every point of the orbit can uniquely be written as

\mathbf{n}=\varepsilon\,\mathbf{s}+2m\mathbf{n}_{0}\,,\qquad\qquad\mathbf{s}\in\mathcal{S}\setminus\{[\mathbf{n}_{0}]\}\,,\quad m\in\mathbb{Z}\,,\quad\varepsilon\in\{-1,1\}\,,

(114)

where
\mathcal{S}:=\mathbb{Z}^{4}/\sim
is the set of classes which distinguish the bi-chains from each other (i.e., it contains only one representative for each one) and indeed the label
\mathbf{s}
is “blind” to both reflections and shifts. Then, we can relabel the orbits (
108
) as
O(\textbf{s})
and split the space of all modes (
112
) as

\mathbb{Z}^{4}=\bigsqcup_{\mathbf{s}\in\mathcal{S}}O(\mathbf{s})\,.

(115)

Clearly, (
114
) is just a convenient relabelling, and we are still considering all possible modes in
\mathbb{Z}^{4}
(except for those in
\mathcal{M}
, which will be addressed below). From now on, every
\mathbf{n}
gets identified by a triple
(\mathbf{s},m,\varepsilon)
where
\mathbf{s}
is the canonical representative
14
14

14

This is just a matter of bookkeeping, standard jargon in set theory. Essentially, for every bi-chain one can identify a unique “anchor” or “origin”, so that
\mathbf{s}
represent that bi-chain only.
for each coset (and tells us on which bi-chain we are),
\varepsilon
specifies whether we are on the “top” or “bottom” row of the bi-chain, and
m
is an integer telling us where exactly we are “along” that bi-chain. For example the field and momentum operators now get new labels:
\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{n}}\rightarrow\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{s},m,\varepsilon}
and
\hat{\pi}_{\mathbf{n}}\rightarrow\hat{\pi}_{\mathbf{s},m,\varepsilon}
. In the new labels, the reality condition
\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{n}}^{\dagger}=\hat{\varphi}_{-\mathbf{n}}
reads
\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{s},m,\varepsilon}^{\dagger}=\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{s},-m,-\varepsilon}
, and the canonical commutator (
9
) becomes
[\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{s},m,\varepsilon},\hat{\pi}_{\mathbf{s^{\prime}},m^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime}}]={\rm i}\delta_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{s^{\prime}}}\delta_{m,m^{\prime}}\delta_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}
.

Importantly, all the terms in the Hamiltonian (
36
) now adopt the
(\mathbf{s},m,\varepsilon)
bookkeeping. Indeed using (
114
) and the property (
105
) one now rewrites the kinetic terms as

K^{(0)}_{\mathbf{n}}\;\rightarrow\;K^{(0)}_{\mathbf{s}}\,,\qquad K^{(2)}_{\mathbf{n}}\;\rightarrow\;K^{(2)}_{\mathbf{s}}\,,

(116)

and, crucially, one finds that the field and momentum modes couple as (using the reality condition)

\displaystyle|\hat{\pi}_{\mathbf{n}}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\hat{\pi}_{\mathbf{n}}\hat{\pi}_{\mathbf{-n}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\;

\displaystyle\hat{\pi}_{\mathbf{s},m,\varepsilon}\hat{\pi}_{\mathbf{s},-m,-\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=|\hat{\pi}_{\mathbf{s},m,\varepsilon}|^{2}\,,

(117)

\displaystyle|\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{n}}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{n}}\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{-n}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\;

\displaystyle\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{s},m,\varepsilon}\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{s},-m,-\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=|\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{s},m,\varepsilon}|^{2}\,,

\displaystyle\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{n}}\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{n}\mp 2\mathbf{n}_{0}}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{n}}\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{-n}\pm 2\mathbf{n}_{0}}

\displaystyle\rightarrow\;

\displaystyle\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{s},m,\varepsilon}\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{s},-m\pm 1,-\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{s},m,\varepsilon}\hat{\varphi}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{s},m\mp 1,\varepsilon}\,.

Explicitly, the relevant contributions to the Hamiltonian (
104
) in these labels can be written as

\hat{H}_{2}^{\cancel{\mathcal{M}}}=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{s},m,\varepsilon}\left[\left(\frac{|\hat{\pi}_{\mathbf{s},m,\varepsilon}|^{2}}{K_{\mathbf{s}}^{(2)}}+K_{\mathbf{s}}^{(0)}|\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{s},m,\varepsilon}|^{2}\right)+\frac{\lambda}{2}\Phi^{2}\big(2|\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{s},m,\varepsilon}|^{2}+\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{s},m,\varepsilon}\hat{\varphi}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{s},m+1,\varepsilon}+\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{s},m,\varepsilon}\hat{\varphi}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{s},m-1,\varepsilon}\big)\right]\,,

(118)

where the notation
\hat{H}_{2}^{\cancel{\mathcal{M}}}
reminds us that this is the Hamiltonian for all non-condensate modes that are not in the special set
\mathcal{M}
(i.e., encompassing all the other bi-chains). It is clear that the only non-diagonal features appearing in the last two terms have been isolated into the integer
m
index. One can see that such labels, say
m
and
m^{\prime}
, are always coupled as (dropping the fixed labels
\mathbf{s}
and
\varepsilon
)

\hat{\varphi}_{m}\delta_{m^{\prime},f(m)}\hat{\varphi}_{m^{\prime}}\,,\qquad f(m)=-m+a\,,\qquad a=\{0\,,1\,,-1\}\,,

(119)

where
f(m)
is an affine involution (essentially the one-dimensional leftover of (
106
)), with
a=0
corresponding to pure reflections. This structure naturally suggests to introduce a one-dimensional discrete Fourier transform along each coset (or Floquet–Bloch transform). Specifically, for each fixed
\mathbf{s}
and
\varepsilon
, we now perform a “chain-Fourier transform” over the label
m\in\mathbb{Z}
as

\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{s},\varepsilon}(\theta):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{e}^{-{\rm i}m\theta}\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{s},m,\varepsilon}\,,\qquad\hat{\pi}_{\mathbf{s},\varepsilon}(\theta):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}\mathrm{e}^{{\rm i}m\theta}\hat{\pi}_{\mathbf{s},m,\varepsilon}\,,

(120)

which naturally introduces a continuous parameter
\theta\in[0\,,2\pi)
called “Bloch angle” in condensed matter literature. The inverse transforms read

\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{s},m,\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathrm{d}\theta\,\mathrm{e}^{{\rm i}m\theta}\,\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{s},\varepsilon}(\theta)\,,\qquad\hat{\pi}_{\mathbf{s},m,\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathrm{d}\theta\,\mathrm{e}^{-{\rm i}m\theta}\,\hat{\pi}_{\mathbf{s},\varepsilon}(\theta)\,.

(121)

Since a Fourier transform is a canonical transformation, one quickly checks that the canonical commutation relations are preserved as
\left[\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{s},\varepsilon}(\theta),\hat{\pi}_{\mathbf{s^{\prime}},\varepsilon^{\prime}}(\theta^{\prime})\right]={\rm i}\delta_{\mathbf{s},\mathbf{s^{\prime}}}\delta(\theta-\theta^{\prime})\delta_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}
; moreover, the reality condition becomes
\hat{\varphi}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{s},\varepsilon}(\theta)=\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{s},-\varepsilon}(\theta)
.

It is easy to see that in this basis the Hamiltonian becomes diagonal. Indeed, if we plug (
121
) into (
118
) we find

\displaystyle\sum_{m}\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{s},m,\varepsilon}\hat{\varphi}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{s},m-a,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=\sum_{m}\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{s},m,\varepsilon}\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{s},-m+a,-\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{{2\pi}}\sum_{m}\int\mathrm{d}\theta\mathrm{d}\theta^{\prime}\,\mathrm{e}^{{\rm i}m\theta}\mathrm{e}^{{\rm i}(a-m)\theta^{\prime}}\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{s},\varepsilon}(\theta)\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{s},-\varepsilon}(\theta^{\prime})

(122)

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}\theta\,\mathrm{e}^{{\rm i}a\theta}\,\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{s},\varepsilon}(\theta)\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{s},-\varepsilon}(\theta)=\int\mathrm{d}\theta\,\mathrm{e}^{{\rm i}a\theta}\,\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{s},\varepsilon}(\theta)\hat{\varphi}^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{s},\varepsilon}(\theta)=\int\mathrm{d}\theta\,\mathrm{e}^{{\rm i}a\theta}\,|\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{s},\varepsilon}(\theta)|^{2}\,,

where
a=\{0\,,1\,,-1\}
, and we used the reality conditions as well as the property
\sum_{m}\mathrm{e}^{{\rm i}m(\theta-\theta^{\prime})}=2\pi\delta(\theta-\theta^{\prime})
.
15
15

15

This holds as a distribution for
2\pi
-periodic functions
f
on
[0\,,2\pi)
satisfying
\int\mathrm{d}\theta^{\prime}f(\theta^{\prime})\sum\mathrm{e}^{{\rm i}m(\theta-\theta^{\prime})}=2\pi f(\theta)
.
Then, assuming that kinetic operators are invariant along each orbit, i.e., using (
116
),
16
16

16

Without property (
116
) the Fourier transform of the kinetic part would read

\sum_{m}\frac{1}{K^{(2)}_{m}}\hat{\pi}_{\mathbf{s},m,\varepsilon}\hat{\pi}_{\mathbf{s},-m,-\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{{2\pi}}\int\mathrm{d}\theta\mathrm{d}\theta^{\prime}\,\hat{\pi}_{\mathbf{s},\varepsilon}(\theta)\hat{\pi}_{\mathbf{s},-\varepsilon}(\theta^{\prime})\sum_{m}\frac{1}{K^{(2)}_{m}}\mathrm{e}^{{\rm i}m(\theta-\theta^{\prime})}\,,

and

\sum_{m}K_{m}^{(0)}\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{s},m,\varepsilon}\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{s},-m,-\varepsilon}=\frac{1}{{2\pi}}\int\mathrm{d}\theta\mathrm{d}\theta^{\prime}\,\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{s},\varepsilon}(\theta)\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{s},-\varepsilon}(\theta^{\prime})\sum_{m}K_{m}^{(0)}\mathrm{e}^{{\rm i}m(\theta-\theta^{\prime})}\,.

Only if the kinetic term does not depend on
m
, one can write the whole Hamiltonian in diagonal form using
\sum_{m}\mathrm{e}^{{\rm i}m(\theta-\theta^{\prime})}=2\pi\delta(\theta-\theta^{\prime})
.
the Hamiltonian becomes

\hat{H}_{2}^{\cancel{\mathcal{M}}}=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{s},\epsilon}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\mathrm{d}\theta\,\left[\frac{|\hat{\pi}_{\mathbf{s},\varepsilon}(\theta)|^{2}}{K_{\mathbf{s}}^{(2)}}+\left(K_{\mathbf{s}}^{(0)}+{\lambda}\Phi^{2}f(\theta)\right)|\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{s},\varepsilon}(\theta)|^{2}\right]\,,

(123)

where
f(\theta)\coloneqq 1+\cos\theta
is the analogue of a “Bloch factor” which appears due to the chain-Fourier transform.

We finally return to the only remaining chain of modes, the severed set
\mathcal{M}
defined in (
113
), which has been excluded from the discussion so far. Since the full quadratic Hamiltonian (
104
) naturally decomposes into independent chain-sectors, we write
\hat{H}_{2}=\hat{H}_{2}^{\mathcal{M}}+\hat{H}_{2}^{\cancel{\mathcal{M}}}
, where
\hat{H}_{2}^{\cancel{\mathcal{M}}}
describes all possible bi-chains except for the set
\mathcal{M}
, as previously noted. To complete the description, we only need to address the last sector
\hat{H}_{2}^{\mathcal{M}}
, for
\mathbf{n}\in\mathcal{M}
. While the procedure largely mirrors our previous treatment, the removal of the condensate labels from
O(\mathbf{n}_{0})
effectively imposes Dirichlet boundary conditions at the ‘ends’ of the severed chain, and will require the use of a sine Fourier transform instead of the standard Fourier transform. In what follows, we quickly show how to diagonalise
\hat{H}_{2}^{\mathcal{M}}
mainly emphasising the minor difference with the methods employed above for the rest of the modes.

Due to the special structure of
\mathcal{M}
(see figure
4
), we introduce the following parametrisation:

\mathbf{n}=\varepsilon(1+2m)\mathbf{n}_{0}\,,\qquad m\in\mathbb{Z}_{>0}\,,\qquad\varepsilon\in\{-1,+1\}\,.

(124)

Every mode
\mathbf{n}\in\mathcal{M}
is then uniquely identified with a pair
(m,\varepsilon)
, where the
positive
integer
m\in\{1,2,3,\dots\}
counts the number of steps in the chain and the binary parameter
\varepsilon
specifies whether we are in the left or right branch (i.e., the overall sign). In these labels, we have
[\hat{\varphi}_{m,\varepsilon},\hat{\pi}_{m^{\prime},\varepsilon^{\prime}}]={\rm i}\delta_{m,m^{\prime}}\delta_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}
and
\hat{\varphi}_{m,\varepsilon}^{\dagger}=\hat{\varphi}_{m,-\varepsilon}
(note that conjugation simply relates the positive branch to the negative branch of the chain, cf. figure
4
). Just like for the rest of the modes, one rewrites the corresponding Hamiltonian

H_{2}^{\mathcal{M}}=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{m,\varepsilon}\left[\left(\frac{|\hat{\pi}_{m,\varepsilon}|^{2}}{K_{\mathcal{M}}^{(2)}}+K_{\mathcal{M}}^{(0)}|\hat{\varphi}_{m,\varepsilon}|^{2}\right)+\frac{\lambda}{2}\Phi^{2}\big(2|\hat{\varphi}_{m,\varepsilon}|^{2}+\hat{\varphi}_{m,\varepsilon}\hat{\varphi}^{\dagger}_{m+\varepsilon,\varepsilon}+\hat{\varphi}_{m,\varepsilon}\hat{\varphi}^{\dagger}_{m-\varepsilon,\varepsilon}\big)\right]\,,

(125)

showing explicitly that the last two terms encode the off-diagonal features in the
m
index (but notice the structure is different than in (
118
)). We now perform a Fourier sine transform for the field operator

\hat{\varphi}_{\varepsilon}(\theta)=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\sin(m \theta)\,\hat{\varphi}_{m,\varepsilon}\,,\qquad\hat{\varphi}_{m,\varepsilon}=\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathrm{d}\theta\,\sin(m\theta)\,\hat{\varphi}_{\varepsilon}(\theta)\,,

(126)

and similarly for the momentum operator, where
\theta\in(0,\pi)
to reflect the vanishing of the basis functions at the boundaries. The transform preserves the canonical commutation relations
[\hat{\varphi}_{\varepsilon}(\theta),\hat{\pi}_{\varepsilon^{\prime}}(\theta^{\prime})]={\rm i}\delta_{\varepsilon,\varepsilon^{\prime}}\delta(\theta-\theta^{\prime})
, and lets us write the reality condition as
\hat{\varphi}^{\dagger}_{\varepsilon}(\theta)=\hat{\varphi}_{-\varepsilon}(\theta)
. By means of the inverse transforms we calculate
17
17

17

Using the completeness relation
\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\sin(m\theta)\sin(m\theta')=\frac{\pi}{2}\delta(\theta-\theta^{\prime})
, which holds as a distribution for
\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in(0,\pi)
, and the identity
\sin\left[(m+\varepsilon)\theta\right]+\sin\left[(m-\varepsilon)\theta\right]=2\sin(m \theta)\cos(\varepsilon\theta)=2\sin(m \theta)\cos(\theta)
, with
\varepsilon=\pm 1
.

\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\hat{\varphi}_{m,\varepsilon}\left(2\hat{\varphi}_{m,-\varepsilon}+\hat{\varphi}_{m+\varepsilon,-\varepsilon}+\hat{\varphi}_{m-\varepsilon,-\varepsilon}\right)=2\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathrm{d}\theta\,(1+\cos\theta)\,\hat{\varphi}_{\varepsilon}(\theta)\hat{\varphi}_{-\varepsilon}(\theta)\,,

(127)

so that (
125
) can be rewritten as

H_{2}^{\mathcal{M}}=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{\pi}\mathrm{d}\theta\,\left[\frac{|\hat{\pi}_{\varepsilon}(\theta)|^{2}}{K_{\mathcal{M}}^{(2)}}+\left(K_{\mathcal{M}}^{(0)}+{\lambda}\Phi^{2}f(\theta)\right)|\hat{\varphi}_{\varepsilon}(\theta)|^{2}\right]\,.

(128)

Finally, we associate a coset label
{\mathbf{s}_{0}}
to the contribution (
128
); this is done by renaming
K_{\mathcal{M}}=K_{\mathbf{s}_{0}}
and by denoting the field and momentum operators for modes in
\mathcal{M}
as
\hat{\varphi}_{{\mathbf{s}_{0}},\varepsilon}(\theta)
and
\hat{\pi}_{{\mathbf{s}_{0}},\varepsilon}(\theta)
. Then, we can include
\hat{H}_{2}^{\mathcal{M}}=\hat{H}_{2}^{\mathbf{s}_{0}}
together with the rest of the cosets contributions (
123
) in the total quadratic Hamiltonian (
104
), obtaining

\hat{H}_{2}=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{s},\epsilon}\int_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{s}}}\mathrm{d}\theta\,\left[\frac{|\hat{\pi}_{\mathbf{s},\varepsilon}(\theta)|^{2}}{K_{\mathbf{s}}^{(2)}}+\left(K_{\mathbf{s}}^{(0)}+{\lambda}\Phi^{2}f(\theta)\right)|\hat{\varphi}_{\mathbf{s},\varepsilon}(\theta)|^{2}\right]\,,

(129)

where
\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{s}}=[0,2\pi)
for all generic bi-chains
\mathbf{s}\neq\mathbf{s}_{0}
, and
\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{s}_{0}}=(0,\pi)
.

In order to lighten the notation, we now define the multi-index
\pm{\bm{\nu}}:=\{\mathbf{s}\,,\theta\,,\pm\varepsilon\}
which conveniently describes out-of-condensate modes in a way that is reminiscent of the old notation for the modes
\mathbf{n\neq\pm n_{0}}
. With this notation, the canonical structure and reality condition read
[\hat{\varphi}_{\bm{\nu}},\hat{\pi}_{\bm{\nu^{\prime}}}]={\rm i}\,\delta_{\bm{\nu},\bm{\nu^{\prime}}}
and
\hat{\varphi}_{\bm{\nu}}^{\dagger}=\hat{\varphi}_{-\bm{\nu}}
, respectively. Also, (
129
) becomes

\hat{H}_{2}=\,\,\,\,\,\mathclap{\displaystyle\int_{\bm{\nu}}}\mathclap{\textstyle\sum\,}\,\,\,\,\hat{H}_{\bm{\nu}}\,,\qquad\hat{H}_{\bm{\nu}}=-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{|\hat{\pi}_{\bm{\nu}}|^{2}}{K_{\bm{\nu}}^{(2)}}+\left(K_{\bm{\nu}}^{(0)}+{\lambda}\Phi^{2}f_{\bm{\nu}}\right)|\hat{\varphi}_{\bm{\nu}}|^{2}\right)\,,

(130)

where we introduced the “sum-integral” notation
\,\,\,\,\,\mathclap{\scalebox{.8}{$\displaystyle\int$}}\hskip 0.5pt\mathclap{\scalebox{.85}{$\textstyle\sum$}\,}\,\,_{\bm{\nu}}:=\sum_{\mathbf{s},\varepsilon}\int_{\mathcal{B}_{\mathbf{s}}}\mathrm{d}\theta
, and we denoted
f_{\bm{\nu}}=f(\theta)
and
K_{\bm{\nu}}=K_{\mathbf{s}}
. This is nothing but (
39
) in the main text.

This Hamiltonian is structurally identical to the free-theory scenario (
103
), with the difference that
K_{\mathbf{n}}^{(0)}
is replaced by the quantity
K_{\bm{\nu}}^{(0)}+{\lambda}\Phi^{2}f_{\bm{\nu}}
.
Of course, by setting
\lambda=0
one recovers the Hamiltonian (
103
), used in section
II
to derive the GFT condensate cosmology results. In fact, the label
\bm{\nu}
itself reduces exactly to
\mathbf{n}
when
\lambda=0
. As we discuss in the main text, this diagonalisation procedure corresponds to a nontrivial Bogolyubov transformation which mixes the atomic ladder operators to define collective excitations.

Appendix C
Validity of neglecting interactions at the background level within GFT condensate cosmology

In this section we carefully assess what conditions have to be satisfied in order to neglect the linear term in the Hamiltonian for the fluctuations (
36
) which arises because we are expanding around a free theory vacuum, instead of expanding around a vacuum of the full theory. We will present the argument first for a generic scalar field theory, and then particularise the conditions for GFT.

Let us start with an action for a real scalar field
\hat{S}[\hat{\varphi}]=\hat{S}_{F}[\hat{\varphi}]+\lambda\hat{S}_{I}[\hat{\varphi}]
. Here
\hat{S}_{\rm F}
is the free theory action, quadratic in the fields, and
\hat{S}_{I}
is a generic interaction term containing beyond quadratic terms in the field, and controlled by the coupling
\lambda
. The dynamics of the theory is found from
\delta\hat{S}/\delta\hat{\varphi}=0
which, taking expectation values for a quantum state of our choice gives

\left\langle{\frac{\delta\hat{S}_{F}}{\delta\hat{\varphi}}}\right\rangle+\lambda\left\langle{\frac{\delta\hat{S}_{I}}{\delta\hat{\varphi}}}\right\rangle=0\,.

(131)

We now take a mean field approximation by replacing
\hat{\varphi}\to\phi_{0}\coloneqq\langle{\hat{\varphi}}\rangle
and we replace expectation values of nonlinear functions of
\hat{\varphi}
by the same functions of
\phi_{0}
, namely and
\langle\hat{F}[\hat{\varphi}]\rangle\to F[\phi_{0}]
. This is a good approximation of (
131
) for quantum states where the fluctuations can be neglected over the mean (e.g., coherent states), and leads to the mean field equations

\left.\frac{\delta S_{F}}{\delta\varphi}+\lambda\frac{\delta S_{I}}{\delta\varphi}\right|_{\varphi=\phi_{0}}=0\,.

(132)

We are interested in computing quantum fluctuations on top of an approximated mean field solution
\varphi_{0}
, which does not solve the above equation, but rather the mean field equations of the free theory, namely (
132
) with
\lambda\to 0
. To do this consistently, we need to make sure that the corrections to the free mean field configuration due to the interaction term are parametrically smaller that the quantum fluctuations on top of the free mean field that we aim to compute. This will occur only in a region of parameter space. To characterise it, let us write the interacting mean field as
\phi_{0}=\varphi_{0}+\delta\varphi_{\lambda}
. Since
\delta\varphi_{\lambda}
vanishes in the limit
\lambda\to 0
, and we are interested in weakly coupled theories where
\lambda
is small, we can plug this ansatz for
\phi_{0}
in (
132
) and expand in powers of
\delta\varphi_{\lambda}
. Solving formally the resulting equation for
\delta\varphi_{\lambda}
leads to

\delta\varphi_{\lambda}=\lambda D_{F}^{-1}J_{I}+O\left({\delta\varphi_{\lambda}^{2},\lambda^{2}}\right)\,,

(133)

where we keep explicit the
\lambda
dependence of neglected terms since we also want to do an expansion in small
\lambda
, and we have defined the effective source
J_{I}
and the differential operators
D_{F}
as

J_{I}\coloneqq-\frac{\delta S_{I}}{\delta\varphi}\Bigg|_{\varphi=\varphi_{0}}\,,\qquad D_{F}\coloneqq\frac{\delta^{2}S_{F}}{\delta\varphi^{2}}\Bigg|_{\varphi=\varphi_{0}}\,.

(134)

Note that we need to choose initial conditions for
\delta\varphi_{\lambda}
to unambiguously define
D_{F}^{-1}
.
In general, we can approximate the true interacting mean field
\phi_{0}
by the free mean field
\varphi_{0}
if
|\delta\varphi_{\lambda}/\varphi_{0}|\ll 1
, namely

\left|\frac{\lambda D_{F}^{-1}J_{I}}{\varphi_{0}}\right|\ll 1\,.

(135)

We now proceed to compute the leading order quantum corrections to
\varphi_{0}
and characterise the regime where
\delta\varphi_{\lambda}
provides subleading contributions. To that end, we write
\hat{\varphi}=\varphi_{0}\hat{\mathbb{I}}+\delta\hat{\varphi}
. Expanding for small fluctuations we find

\hat{S}[\hat{\varphi}]=S[\varphi_{0}]\hat{\mathbb{I}}+\left.\frac{\delta S}{\delta\varphi}\right|_{\varphi=\varphi_{0}}\delta\hat{\varphi}+\frac{1}{2}\left.\frac{\delta^{2}S}{\delta\varphi^{2}}\right|_{\varphi=\varphi_{0}}\delta\hat{\varphi}^{2}+O(\delta\hat{\varphi}^{3})\,,

(136)

and the linear dynamics for small quantum fluctuations around
\varphi_{0}
is then given by

(D_{F}+\lambda D_{I})\delta\hat{\varphi}=\lambda J_{I}\hat{\mathbb{I}}+O(\delta\hat{\varphi}^{2})\,,

(137)

where we have defined the operator

D_{I}\coloneqq\frac{\delta^{2}S_{I}}{\delta\varphi^{2}}\Bigg|_{\varphi=\varphi_{0}}\,.

(138)

If
\varphi_{0}
was an exact solution of the interacting mean field equations, the source term on the right hand side would vanish exactly. However, since it is only a solution of the free theory, we have
{\delta S}/{\delta\varphi}|_{\varphi=\varphi_{0}}=-\lambda J_{I}
and the source term remains. We can now split the quantum fluctuations into a homogeneous piece and a sourced piece
\delta\hat{\varphi}=\delta\hat{\varphi}_{h}+\delta\hat{\varphi}_{\lambda}
, such that

\displaystyle\left({D_{F}+\lambda D_{I}}\right)\delta\hat{\varphi}_{h}=O(\delta\hat{\varphi}_{h}^{2},\delta\hat{\varphi}_{h}\delta\hat{\varphi}_{\lambda})\,,

(139)

\displaystyle D_{F}\delta\hat{\varphi}_{\lambda}=\lambda J_{I}\hat{\mathbb{I}}+O\left({\delta\hat{\varphi}_{\lambda}^{2},\lambda^{2}}\right)\,.

(140)

Note that
\delta\hat{\varphi}_{\lambda}
is exactly the contribution to the quantum corrections that we want to neglect, which comes from the fact that we are expanding around an approximated mean field (the free mean field
\varphi_{0}
) instead of the true mean field
\phi_{0}
. Furthermore, we see that to leading order
\delta\hat{\varphi}_{\lambda}=\delta\varphi_{\lambda}\hat{\mathbb{I}}
, where
\delta\varphi_{\lambda}
is the difference between the free mean field and the true interacting mean field. A sufficient condition for the existence of a regime in which
\delta\hat{\varphi}_{\lambda}
can be neglected while meaningfully accounting for
\delta\hat{\varphi}_{h}
is given by
\sqrt{\langle{\delta\hat{\varphi}_{\lambda}^{2}}\rangle}\ll\sqrt{\langle{\delta\hat{\varphi}_{h}^{2}}\rangle}
, which we can write as

\left|\frac{{\lambda}D_{F}^{-1}J_{I}}{\sqrt{\langle{\delta\hat{\varphi}_{h}^{2}}\rangle}}\right|\ll 1\,.

(141)

If the mean field approximation holds (i.e.,
|\sqrt{\langle{\delta\hat{\varphi}^{2}}\rangle}/\varphi_{0}|\ll 1
), the above condition is more restrictive than the one required for the interacting mean field to be well approximated by the free mean field (
135
).

We now want to particularise these results to our GFT with a quartic interaction potential. We start with the GFT action expanded in modes (
92
) and the interaction potential (
34
). Adapting the above formalism to the case of the real-valued GFT field, with complex modes satisfying
\varphi_{-\mathbf{n}}=\varphi_{\mathbf{n}}^{*}
, we write the free mean field as
\varphi_{0}=\varphi_{\pm\mathbf{n}_{0}}\to\Phi
and

\displaystyle D_{F}

\displaystyle\to D_{F}^{\mathbf{nm}}(\chi,\chi^{\prime})=\delta_{\mathbf{nm}}\left({K^{(0)}_{\mathbf{n}}+K^{(2)}_{\mathbf{n}}\partial_{\chi}^{2}}\right)\delta(\chi-\chi^{\prime})\,,

(142)

\displaystyle J_{I}

\displaystyle\to J_{I}^{\mathbf{n}}(\chi)=-\frac{\Phi^{3}(\chi)}{2}\left[{\delta_{\mathbf{n},{\mathbf{n}}_{0}}+\delta_{\mathbf{n},-\mathbf{n}_{0}}+\frac{1}{3}\delta_{\mathbf{n},3\mathbf{n}_{0}}+\frac{1}{3}\delta_{\mathbf{n},-3\mathbf{n}_{0}}}\right]\,.

In our case
J_{I}^{\mathbf{n}}
vanishes for
\mathbf{n}\notin\{\pm\mathbf{n}_{0},\pm 3\mathbf{n}_{0}\}
, and it depends on relational time only through the GFT mean field
\Phi(\chi)=\sqrt{2N_{\text{c}}(\chi)/\Omega_{\mathbf{n}_{0}}}
.

We now need to find the unique solution to the corresponding differential equation,

\left({K^{(0)}_{\mathbf{n}}+K^{(2)}_{\mathbf{n}}\partial_{\chi}^{2}}\right)\delta\varphi^{\mathbf{n}}_{\lambda}(\chi)=\lambda J_{I}^{\mathbf{n}}(\chi)\,,

(143)

with initial conditions dictated by (
31
) (this fixes the ambiguity in the definition of
D_{F}^{-1}
mentioned above). To that end, we apply Duhamel’s principle to find the unique solutions for each mode (cf. (
15
)):

\delta\varphi^{\mathbf{n}}_{\lambda}=\frac{\lambda}{K_{\mathbf{n}}^{(2)}\omega_{\mathbf{n}}}\int_{0}^{\chi}S_{\mathbf{n}}(\chi-t)J_{I}^{\mathbf{n}}(t)\mathrm{d}t\,,\qquad S_{\mathbf{n}}(\chi-t)=\begin{cases}\sin(\omega_{\bn}(\chi-t))\,,&\text{for $\mathbf{n}\in\mathfrak{N}^{\text{HO}}$}\,,\\
\sinh(\omega_{\bn}(\chi-t))\,,&\text{for $\mathbf{n}\in\mathfrak{N}^{\text{SQ}}$}\,,\end{cases}

(144)

where
\omega_{\mathbf{n}}
is defined in (
14
). Then, according to (
142
), since the only modes that are sourced are
\pm{\mathbf{n}}_{0}
and
\pm 3{\mathbf{n}}_{0}
, the only relevant upper bounds are given by

|\delta\varphi_{\lambda}^{\pm\mathbf{n}_{0}}|\leq\lambda\frac{\Phi^{3}(\chi)}{2}d_{F}^{\mathbf{n}_{0}}(\chi)\,,\qquad|\delta\varphi_{\lambda}^{\pm 3\mathbf{n}_{0}}|\leq\lambda\frac{\Phi^{3}(\chi)}{6}d_{F}^{3\mathbf{n}_{0}}(\chi)\,,

(145)

where we have used that
\Phi(\chi)
is positive and monotonically growing for
\chi>0
, and we have defined

d^{\mathbf{n}}_{F}(\chi)=\frac{1}{|K^{(2)}_{\mathbf{n}}|\omega_{\mathbf{n}}}\int_{0}^{\chi}|S_{\mathbf{n}}(\chi-t)|\mathrm{d}t=\frac{1}{|K_{\mathbf{n}}^{(0)}|}\times\begin{cases}{2T^{\mathbf{n}}_{2}(\chi)+1-\cos\big({\omega_{\bn}\chi-\pi T^{\bn}_2(\chi)}\big)}\,,&\text{for $\mathbf{n}\in\mathfrak{N}^{\text{HO}}$}\,,\\
{\cosh(\omega_{\bn}\chi)-1}\,,&\text{for $\mathbf{n}\in\mathfrak{N}^{\text{SQ}}$}\,,\end{cases}

(146)

with
T^{\mathbf{n}}_{2}(\chi)=\lfloor\omega_{\mathbf{n}}\chi/\pi\rfloor
the floor (or integer part) of
\omega_{\mathbf{n}}\chi/\pi
(which basically counts the number of half-oscillation periods of the sine function over the integration interval
[0,\chi]
). Note that the function
d_{F}^{\mathbf{n}}
essentially grows linearly with
\chi
for HO modes, whereas it grows exponentially in
\chi
for SQ modes.

By means of (
33
), we can finally write the conditions under which using of the free mean field is a valid approximation in terms of the GFT parameters and the number of condensed quanta. By requiring that
|\delta\varphi_{\lambda}^{\pm\mathbf{n}_{0}}|\ll\Phi(\chi)
and
|\delta\varphi_{\lambda}^{\pm 3\mathbf{n}_{0}}|\ll\Phi(\chi)
, we conclude that the free mean field is a good approximation to the interacting mean field at relational time
\chi
provided the following conditions hold:

\left|\frac{d_{F}^{\mathbf{n}_{0}}(\chi)}{\Omega_{\mathbf{n}_{0}}}\lambda N_{\text{c}}(\chi)\right|\ll 1\,,\qquad\left|\frac{d_{F}^{3\mathbf{n}_{0}}(\chi)}{3\Omega_{\mathbf{n}_{0}}}\lambda N_{\text{c}}(\chi)\right|\ll 1\,.

(147)

Moreover, from
|\delta\varphi_{\lambda}^{\pm 3\mathbf{n}_{0}}|\ll\sqrt{\langle{\hat{\varphi}_{\pm 3\mathbf{n}_{0}}^{2}(\chi)}\rangle}
we find that quantum fluctuations on top of the mean field can be computed while neglecting
\delta\hat{\varphi}_{\lambda}^{\mathbf{n}}
if

\left|\frac{\sqrt{2}}{3{\Omega_{\mathbf{n}_{0}}^{3/2}}}\frac{d_{F}^{3\mathbf{n}_{0}}(\chi)}{\sqrt{\langle{\hat{\varphi}_{\pm 3\mathbf{n}_{0}}^{2}(\chi)}\rangle}}\lambda N_{\text{c}}^{3/2}(\chi)\right|\ll 1\,.

(148)

Notice how the conditions (
147
) modify the standard diluteness condition
|\lambda|N_{\text{c}}(\chi)\ll 1
with an integral function
d_{F}(\chi)
, which essentially captures how much the free-theory trajectory deviates from the true interacting mean field as a function of time. It is interesting to also note that since the condensate mode
\mathbf{n}_{0}
is of SQ type (and is assumed to have the largest squeezing rate),
d_{F}^{\mathbf{n}_{0}}(\chi)
outpaces
d_{F}^{3\mathbf{n}_{0}}(\chi)
at late times, regardless of whether the mode
3\mathbf{n}_{0}
is of SQ or HO type. As a result, the first condition in (
147
) is the more restrictive one asymptotically, while the second is subleading (and redundant at late times).

Condition (
148
), on the other hand, imposes a different physical constraint. Since the interactions act as a source for the modes
\pm 3\mathbf{n}_{0}
, whose mean-field background vanishes, (
148
) is simply a requirement that the the sourced contribution remains smaller than the intrinsic quantum fluctuations of these modes.
